3.4. Study of ODEs. As explained in § 3.2, throughout this section we study the weak ODE
system (69) below on the bootstrap interval [0, TB (A, m)], using the bootstrap bounds (65). The
constants introduced in this subsection may depend on the fixed background parameters from § 1.13,
but they are independent of the running parameters k, m, A, t, TN , TB unless such dependence is
displayed explicitly. In particular, the constants cµ , Cµ , and Θµ are fixed once introduced. See
§ 1.13 for more notation conventions.
                   SHARP ILL-POSEDNESS OF THE EULER EQUATIONS IN LORENTZ SPACES                                         45


   We use superscript/subscript, such as orig, loc, froz, to distinguish various types of simplifi-
cations. In addition, we will denote xorig
                                        k (t) := xk (t) throughout this Section 3.4, but after this
section, we will come back to the original notation xk (t).

Roadmap of this subsection. As already foreshadowed in the introduction, the purpose of this
subsection is to isolate the ODE cascade that drives norm inflation.
       • § 3.4.1: On the bootstrap interval, we first use the flow-map control to freeze the transported
         profiles Wj against the fixed profile ϕq , thereby reducing the weak ODEs to outer-ring
         contributions depending essentially on the amplitudes xj and aspect ratios Γj .
       • § 3.4.2: We then rewrite the system in cascade form, which yields an exact identity for the
         evolution of the Γj ’s, and then we compare the frozen and unfrozen quantities.
       • § 3.4.3: After introducing the cone variables ζj = Lq Γj , we exploit the monotonicity of
         the localized Biot–Savart kernel on a fixed productive range: while the front moves toward
         smaller scales, the cumulative outer stretching must build up at a quantitative rate.
       • § 3.4.4, § 3.4.5: This front-migration mechanism gives a front-induced t−1 lower bound for
         the total stretching, and from there we derive an upper bound for the ODE norm-inflation
         time. These ODE estimates are the main output of the subsection and will be used in the
         next subsection to close the bootstrap.

3.5. A bootstrap Argument. In this subsection, fix A > ε(ε̃, q). For m ≥ 2, we work on the
time interval [0, TB (A, m)] so that we can use the bootstrap bounds in (65). In addition, we use
the norm inflation at the ODE level, Proposition 3.6, that is, TB (A, m) → 0 as m → ∞, in order
to improve the bootstrap bounds in (65).
   The argument has three steps:
    (1) Confinement for Wk and scale separation of supp ωk (·, t) (Lemmas 3.7, 3.8).
    (2) Estimates on velocity-error Vk (Lemma 3.9).
    (3) Integration along characteristics and bootstrap improvement (Lemma 3.10).
   From this point on, ODE simplifications such as localization and freezing are no longer essential,
so we return to the original notation xk (t) and Γk (t) instead of xorig       orig
                                                                    k (t) and Γk (t).

3.5.1. Confinement and Scale Separation.
Lemma 3.7 (Confinement from Xk ). Fix k ∈ {1, . . . , m}. For every t ∈ [0, TB (A, m)],
                                                                             

where

xk (t)Wk (r/Rk (t), z/Hk (t), t), which gives the confinement of supp ωk (·, t).                             □

3.5.2. Velocity-Error Estimates. The goal of this subsection is to estimate the rescaled velocity

from the k-th ring itself.

there exists δ̃ > 0 such that Fm (t) < µ on [0, TB + δ̃], contradicting maximality of TB . Therefore,
TB = TN .                                                                                           □

                                       4. Instantaneous blow-up
     This section is devoted to establishing Theorem 1.2. This section is organized as follows:
       • We first prove preliminary lemmas for Yudovich-type weak solutions (u, ω). (§ 4.1).
       • Using the preliminary lemmas, we will prove the non-existence result, Theorem 1.2-(1)
          (§ 4.2).
       • Next, we will prove the instantaneous blow-up result, Theorem 1.2-(2) by taking the limit
          of the sequence of our finite-ring approximations (§ 4.3).
4.1. Preliminary Lemmas for Instantaneous Blow-Up. This subsection is devoted to prelim-
inary lemmas: primitive-variable formulation (Lemma 4.1), uniqueness (Lemma 4.2), a flow map
and the Cauchy formula (Lemma 4.3) and a Danskin-type lemma (Lemma 4.4).
   We will use these lemmas in the proof of Theorem 1.2-(1) as follows: Fix T0 > 0 and assume
for contradiction that there exists a Yudovich-type weak solution (u, ω) on (0, T0 ). We first derive
primitive-variable and uniqueness consequences of this assumption, then use them to propagate the
symmetry and sign structure, and finally run a finite-head bootstrap on the first M rings while
treating the tail by Proposition 2.1, the flow map lemma and the Danskin-type lemma.
   We prove Lemmas 4.1, 4.2 in this subsection, but the proof of Lemma 4.4 is deferred to Appen-
dix A, which appears after the proofs of Theorem 1.2 so that the main proofs are not interrupted.
   For instantaneous blow-up, we argue by contradiction, which leads to the existence of a bounded
solution in a time interval.
   As opposed to the proof for norm inflation, continuity of the bootstrap deviation functional
Fm (t) breaks down when m = ∞; in this case, the supremum over infinitely many indices k in the
                 SHARP ILL-POSEDNESS OF THE EULER EQUATIONS IN LORENTZ SPACES                               59


definition (65) of Fm (t) prevents the functional from being continuous. It is not obvious how to
make a bootstrap argument for infinitely many rings all together.
  Instead, for infinitely many rings k = 1, 2, . . . , we make a bootstrap argument only for finitely
many rings k = 1, . . . , M . Hence in the proof below, M denotes the number of rings for which we
make a bootstrap argument.
Primitive-Variable Formulation. The next lemma isolates the passage from the weak formu-
lation of the relative vorticity equation to the primitive-variable Euler formulation. The Lorentz
exponent q plays no role here; only the weak transport identity (19) and the uniform L1 ∩ L∞
bound are used.

Uniqueness. To prove preservation of odd-symmetry and non-positivity in the upper half space,
we need uniqueness beyond Danchin’s critical regime for Yudovich-type weak solutions (u, ω),
which satisfy ω ∈ L∞ ((0, T0 ); L1 ∩ L∞ (R3 )). The proof adapts the classical strategy of Yudovich’s
uniqueness result for two-dimensional flows (see [Yud63, Section 3] and [MB01, Theorem 8.2]); see
also [Dan07b, Theorem 1.2]. Although the argument is a straightforward generalization of the 2D
case, we provide a precise statement and full proof for the sake of completeness. In the proof, we
will use a primitive-variable formulation proved in Lemma 4.1.

Flow map and Cauchy formula under bounded vorticity. Next, we prove the existence of a
flow map and the Cauchy formula under bounded vorticity with a velocity field of finite energy. This
lemma implies that as we consider bounded vorticity, it yields ∇u ∈ BM O, hence u is log-Lipschitz.
Then the Lagrangian transport of ξ = ω/r is available.
   We will use this lemma for both (1) and (2) of Theorem 1.2.
Lemma 4.3 (Flow map and Cauchy formula under bounded vorticity). Let T0 > 0. Let u be
divergence-free and axisymmetric without swirl on (0, T0 ) × R3 , and let ω0 = ω0 (r, z), ω = ω(r, z, t)

Danskin-Type Lemma. Next, we record a Danskin-type lemma, which will be used to handle
rings closer to the origin. This lemma is likely known in related forms, but as we could not find a
statement that exactly fits our situation, we include it here for completeness. This is an absolutely-
continuous-in-time envelope lemma tailored to our Lagrangian radial extrema, rather than a direct
invocation of the classical smooth Danskin theorem (See [Dan67], [BR95]).
Lemma 4.4 (Danskin-type lemma). Fix T0 > 0, and let K ⊂ RN be compact, and let
                                             Y r : K × [0, T0 ] → R
be continuous. Assume that there exists a bounded function
                                             g : K × (0, T0 ) → R
measurable in t for each fixed x ∈ K, such that, for every x ∈ K and every t ∈ [0, T0 ],
                                                      Z t
                                  r         r
                                Y (x, t) = Y (x, 0) +     g(x, s) ds.
                                                                0

Assume furthermore that there exists a full-measure set Eg ⊂ (0, T0 ) and a modulus of continuity
ρ : [0, ∞) → [0, ∞) with lims→0+ ρ(s) = 0, such that
                      |g(x, t) − g(y, t)| ≤ ρ(|x − y|)         for all x, y ∈ K, t ∈ Eg .       (114)
  Define
                           RK (t) := max Y r (x, t),          rK (t) := min Y r (x, t),
                                       x∈K                              x∈K
and
                      MK (t) := argmax Y r (x, t),            AK (t) := argmin Y r (x, t).
                                      x∈K                                  x∈K

The definition of argmaxx∈K       Y r (x, t) is as follows:
                         argmax Y r (x, t) = {y ∈ K : Y r (y, t) = max Y r (x, t)},
                            x∈K                                         x∈K

and we similarly define argminx∈K Y r (x, t). Then RK and rK are Lipschitz on [0, T0 ]. Moreover,
there exists a full-measure set E ⊂ Eg such that for every t ∈ E
                          ′                                    ′
                         RK (t) =      max g(x, t),           rK (t) = min g(x, t).
                                     x∈MK (t)                          x∈AK (t)

In particular, these identities hold for a.e. t ∈ (0, T0 ).
  A proof of this Lemma 4.4 is written in Appendix A.

Hierarchy of index thresholds. Throughout the proof, we introduce several integer thresholds
depending on (A, q):
              m0 (A, q),      mcone (A, q),      mtail (A, q),     m1 (A, q),       m2 (A, q).
For clarity, we summarize their roles and dependencies.
     (i) m0 (A, q) ensures scale separation for the first M rings (Lemma 3.8). In particular, for all
         M ≥ m0 (A, q), the supports {supp ωk }M   k=1 are pairwise disjoint and satisfy the geometric
         separation bounds (91)–(92). See (118) and the paragraph following Proposition 4.5.
                                                                                         (∞)
    (ii) mcone (A, q) ≥ m0 (A, q) guarantees that all trajectories starting from supp ω0 remain in
         the cone region Lr ≥ |z| on [0, TB (A, M )] for M ≥ mcone (A, q). This  √ allows us to use
         Proposition 2.1 uniformly in time with the factor |x|/rx bounded by 1 + L2 . See (122).
  (iii) mtail (A, q) ≥ mcone (A, q) ensures strict separation between the head (k ≤ M ) and the tail
         (k > M ), namely
                                 rM,− (t)
                                           ≥ 4 for all t ∈ [0, TB (A, M )],
                                 Rtail (t)
         which yields the uniform distance bound (125) and allows us to control the tail contribution.
         See (124).
   (iv) m1 (A, q) ≥ mtail (A, q) is chosen large enough so that
                                                           
                              CE Ā 1 + log(Ā/ε) + log M TB (A, M ) ≪ 1,
       which enables the bootstrap improvement and yields TB (A, M ) = TN (A, M ). See (128).
   (v) m2 (A, q) ≥ m1 (A, q) is chosen so that the norm-inflation time satisfies
                                              TN (A, M ) < T0
       for all M ≥ m2 (A, q). This ensures that the ODE norm inflation occurs within the pre-
       scribed time interval and allows us to evaluate the solution at t = TN (A, M ).
  In particular, for all M ≥ m2 (A, q), all the above properties hold simultaneously.
Step 1: Finite–ring bootstrap (tail handled by the contradiction hypothesis).
  For any integer M ≥ 2, define TN (A, M ) and TB (A, M ) by
                                                                
                TN (A, M ) := sup t ∈ [0, T0 ] : max xk (t) ≤ Ā
                                                    1≤k≤M
                  TB (A, M ) := sup {T ∈ [0, TN (A, M )] : FM (t) ≤ µ all t ∈ [0, T ]} .
where the bootstrap functional FM (t) is defined in (65). Since M is finite and the function (r, z, t) 7→
Xk (r, z, t) is continuous, FM (t) is continuous and FM (0) = 0, hence TB (A, M ) > 0.
   By the definition, TB (A, M ) ≤ TN (A, M ), hence xk (t) ≤ Ā on [0, TB (A, M )].
   Before we state the main goal of Step 1, by using the bootstrap bounds on [0, TB (A, M )] and
the fact that xk (t) ≤ Ā on [0, TB (A, M )], we prove confinement and scale-separation for the head
rings in the same way as in the proof for norm inflation.
Proposition 4.5 (Finite-head reduction for the first M rings). The following statements hold on
[0, TB (A, M )] for the first M rings.
     (i) For every 1 ≤ k ≤ M , the Cauchy formula (117) holds and
                   (1 − µ)∥ϕq ∥L∞ (R3 ) ≤ ∥Wk (·, ·, t)∥L∞ (R3 ) ≤ (1 + µ)∥ϕq ∥L∞ (R3 ) ,
                 ∥ωk (·, t)∥L∞ (R3 ) ≤ (1 + µ)xk (t)∥ϕq ∥L∞ (R3 ) ≤ (1 + µ)Ā∥ϕq ∥L∞ (R3 ) .

           Consequently, repeating the proof of Proposition 3.6 with TB := TB (A, M ) gives
                                                           C(q, A)

                                                                 r−

  Since TB (A, M ) → 0 as M → ∞, there exists a large integer m0 (A, q) such that for all M ≥
m0 (A, q), the inequality (118) holds. Hence by Proposition 4.5, (91), (92) hold for all M ≥ m0 (A, q).
Proof of Proposition 4.5. The pointwise Cauchy formula (117) was established above directly from
the global flow map Y and the definition of ωk ; in particular, it remains valid in the contradiction
setting and does not rely on Lemma 3.1. Combining (117) with the bootstrap bound FM (t) ≤ µ
gives the L∞ bounds in part (i).
   Next fix 2 ≤ k ≤ M . The ODE
                                          x′k (t) = xk (t) ∂r ur− (0, 0, t)
depends only on the rings with indices j < k ≤ M . Therefore the proofs of Lemma 3.2 and the
weak ODE bounds (71) in Section 3.4 apply without change to the family {ωj }M j=1 on [0, TB (A, M )].
Since TB (A, M ) ≤ TN (A, M ), we have xk (t) ≤ Ā for all 1 ≤ k ≤ M and 0 ≤ t ≤ TB (A, M ). Thus,
because it only uses the first k − 1 rings and the finite-head bootstrap FM ≤ µ, the proof of
Proposition 3.6 yields the bound in (ii).
   Finally, Lemma 3.7 depends only on the bootstrap control of Xk , and Lemma 3.8 uses Lemma 3.7,
the ODE (60), and the bound xk (t) ≤ Ā on [0, TB (A, M )]. Hence the same proofs give (iii) for the
first M rings. It finishes the proof of Proposition 4.5.                                          □
     The goal of Step 1 is to prove, using a bootstrap argument, that for large M,
                                           TB (A, M ) = TN (A, M ).                                         (119)
Assume for contradiction that TB (A, M ) < TN (A, M ).

Remark 4.2. In Proposition 4.8, we upgrade the distributional solution furnished by Corollary 4.7
to a Yudovich-type weak solution provided the distributional solution is uniformly bounded. One
may not upgrade any uniformly bounded distributional solution to a Yudovich-type weak solution.
In that case, the corresponding velocity field u may not be uniformly bounded. Then the associated
flow map might escape to infinity in finite time.
                                         (∞)
Proof of Theorem 1.2-(2). Let ω0 be the same initial data as in the first paragraph of the proof
for non-existence (§ 4.2). Let (u(∞) , ω (∞) ) be the global distributional solution furnished by Corol-
lary 4.7. We claim that
                                  / L∞ (0, T ) × R3

                                                      
                                                            for every T > 0.
Indeed, if this were false, then for some T > 0 we would have
                                       ω (∞) ∈ L∞ (0, T ) × R3 .
                                                              

Applying Proposition 4.8 to the restriction of (u(∞) , ω (∞) ) to [0, T ], we would obtain
                                 ω (∞) ∈ L∞ 0, T ; L1 ∩ L∞ (R3 ) ,
                                                                    

so that (u(∞) , ω (∞) ) is a Yudovich-type weak solution on (0, T ). This contradicts Theorem 1.2-(1).
Therefore the claim is proved, and Theorem 1.2-(2) follows.                                         □

       Appendix A. Proof of the Danskin-type lemma and the Cauchy formula

   For the Cauchy formula, we first state a proposition before proving it.

reference that fits our situation. Hence, we prove them here in this appendix for completeness.

                                          Appendix B. ODE Models
   This appendix records the two auxiliary ODE analyses announced in § 1.8. We retain the notation
introduced there and only recall the definitions needed below. These propositions are not used in
the proof of the main theorems; they serve only as to explain motivations behind the localized
model. This appendix provides rigorous justifications of the heuristic discussion in § 1.8.2 and
§ 1.8.3.

                                     Appendix C. List of notation
   For the convenience of the reader, we collect here the main notation that recurs throughout
the paper. This appendix is intended as a global lookup table for symbols used repeatedly in the
statements and main proofs. Proof-local symbols and locally introduced constants are usually not
listed here.
      • Biot–Savart law and sign convention. We define vorticity by curl u = ω in the general
        case and curl u = ωeθ in the axisymmetric, no-swirl case. This determines the signs in
        the Biot–Savart law as indicated in (3) and (5), and we will use these definitions and the
        Biot–Savart laws with these signs. For the correct sign of the general Biot–Savart law,
        see [EGP18, the first page] and [Tsa18, Equation (1.44)].
94                             JEAHEANG BANG AND ALEXEY CHESKIDOV


          On the other hand, if one defines vorticity with the minus sign, that is, curl u = −ω
       in the general case and curl u = −ωeθ in the axisymmetric, no-swirl case. Then it will
       lead to an extra minus sign in the Biot–Savart law. This convention is used for example
       in [KJ22, Equations (1.3), (1.5), (1.6)].
          The displayed Biot–Savart formulas in [CMZ25, Equation (3)], [MB01, Equations (2.94),
       (2.95)], [BV22, Equation (1.53)], and [Dan07a, p. 477] use the cross-product order (x − y) ×
       ω(y). This is the negative of the convention used in the present paper. Consequently, those
       displayed Biot–Savart formulas correspond to the replacement ωref = −ω when compared
       with the present convention.
     • Outer-region notation for Proposition 2.1 (Section 2).
          – Q(ρ) := {y ∈ R3 : ry ≥ 2ρ}: the outer region with radial cutoff ρ in Proposition 2.1.
     • Physical variables and coordinates.
          – u = ur er + uz ez : the axisymmetric no-swirl velocity field.
          – curl u = ω(r, z, t)eθ : the vorticity vector where ω denotes the scalar angular vorticity.
          – ξ := ω/r: the relative vorticity.
          – (r, θ, z): cylindrical coordinates in R3 .
          – (rx , θx , zx ): the cylindrical coordinates of a point x ∈ R3 .
     • Parameter hierarchy and fixed background data.
          – ε̃ > 0, q > 1: the fundamental parameters.
          – Lq , αq , ϕq , z0,q : quantities fixed once q is fixed.
                  ∗ Lq = z0,q /r0 is the cone-slope parameter.
                  ∗ αq is the logarithmic decay exponent.
                  ∗ ϕq is the fixed reference profile.
                  ∗ z0,q is the corresponding reference height.
          – r0 , η, µ, d: globally fixed geometric parameters. In the paper they are taken to be
                                                  1             1
                               r0 = 1,       η= ,         µ= ,           d = 10−2 .
                                                  4            20
          – ε = ε(ε̃, q): the amplitude parameter chosen after fixing (ε̃, q) and thereafter treated
            as fixed.
          – k, m, A, t, TN , TB : running parameters.
          – k: the ring index; smaller k corresponds to a larger, more outer ring.
          – m: the total number of rings in the finite superposition.
     • Initial data and multiring decomposition (mainly Section 3.1 and Section 4).
               (m)
          – ω0 = m
                       P
                           k=1 ω0,k : the finite-ring initial data; in the instantaneous blow-up argument
                                                    (∞)
            one also uses the infinite sum ω0 = ∞
                                                           P
                                                              k=1 ω0,k .
               (m)                                                         (m)
          – ω : the smooth finite-ring solution generated by ω0 .
          – ωk : the evolved k-th vortex ring.
          – u(m) : the velocity induced by ω (m) .
          – uk : the velocity induced by ωk .
          – For a fixed k, ω− := k−1          ωj and u− := k−1
                                       P                       P
                              Pm          j=1           Pm       j=1 uj denote the outer-ring contributions,
            while ω+ := j=k+1 ωj and u+ := j=k+1 uj denote the inner-ring contributions.
                               (m)      (m)
        – ξ (m) := ω (m) /r, ξ0 := ω0 /r: relative-vorticity variables.
     • Rescaled ring variables and local flow maps (Section 3.1).
        – xk (t): the amplitude factor of the k-th ring.
        – x̃k (t) := xk (t)/xk (0): the normalized amplitude.
           SHARP ILL-POSEDNESS OF THE EULER EQUATIONS IN LORENTZ SPACES                           95


    – Rk (t) = dk x̃k (t): the radial scale of the k-th ring.
    – Hk (t) = dk x̃k (t)−2 : the vertical scale of the k-th ring.
    – Wk (r, z, t): the rescaled profile of the k-th ring, defined through
                                                                          
                                                            r      z
                            ωk (r, z, t) = xk (t) Wk             ,       ,t .

   – Vk = (Vkr , Vkz ): the rescaled velocity field driving Wk .
   – Xk = (Xkr , Xkz ): the flow map associated with Vk on the support of ϕq .
• Bootstrap quantities (Section 3.2 and § 3.5).
   – A > 0: the target amplitude for norm inflation.
   – Ā := A/(1 − µ): the renormalized target amplitude.
   – TN (A, m): the ODE norm-inflation time, i.e. the time at which some xk reaches Ā.
   – Fm (t): the bootstrap deviation functional measuring the relative distortion of Xkr /r
     and Xkz /z on supp ϕq .
   – TB (A, m): the maximal time on which the bootstrap bounds Fm (t) ≤ µ hold.
• ODE/stretching cascade notation (Section 3.4; see also § 1.8 and Appendix B).
   – Superscript/subscript convention.
          ∗ Superscript orig denotes quantities belonging to the original weak ODEs, i.e. the
            PDE-driven cascade with the true coefficients Λj (t, Γ).
          ∗ Subscript froz means that the transported profile has been frozen against the
            fixed profile ϕq , so Λj (t, Γ) is replaced by Λfroz (Γ) while the amplitude and aspect-
            ratio variables remain those of the original cascade.
          ∗ Superscript loc denotes the localized model, where Λfroz (Γ) is replaced by the
            explicit localized coefficient Λloc (Γ).
          ∗ In the heuristic discussion and Appendix B, superscripts such as froz, flat, and
            str denote the stand-alone frozen-profile, flattened-coefficient, and strong no-self-
            slowdown model ODEs, respectively.
   – In Section 3.4 we temporarily write xorig      k (t) := xk (t) (and similarly for the derived
     ODE quantities) in order to distinguish the original cascade from its simplified models.
     After that section, the paper returns to the shorter notation xk (t).
